Weights moved: weak-side and strong-side linebackers, corners and nickel backs down; safeties and defensive tackles up; middle linebacker unchanged (so the leaders stay put). After: per-game rates match real for every role (corners 4.8, ends 3.1, tackles 3.2, weak-side LBs 6.4). **12+ tackle games ~181-188 -> ~160-170 a season** (real 151, range 140-160); **15+ ~35-38 -> 30-37** (real 31); 10+ 482 -> ~435 (real 405, 366-463); big games now come from linebackers 125 / safeties 29 / corners 6 (real 114 / 27 / ~6; corners had 16-19). Season: 80+ tackle players 124 -> 113-116 (real 102), 80+ tackle DBs 65 -> 57-59 (real 46), tackles#100 back in range, league leader 185 (183). Season check: 20 of 282. Defensive-line tackle leaderboards now read above the real "DL"-labeled ones (10th best 68 vs 63, 50+ tackle linemen 48 vs 34); by role (real line + edge linebackers) the real numbers are 66-72 and 38-60, so it matches; `tools/measures.py` now marks `dl_tackles` with that known reason, like edge sacks.
- **Change 3, running back workload** (`tuning.RB_CARRIES_STEP` 0.10 -> 0.03). After ~16 carries the staff handed the backup 10% more of the snaps per carry, so every back's day flattened to 16-22 carries: 25+ carry games 12.5 a season (real 31.8), 30+ 0.2 (6.2). Now the brake is gentle and workhorses swing from game to game like real ones (game-to-game spread 4.9-5.0 carries vs real 4.8-5.8). **150-yard rushing games 13.0 -> 15.8 (real 16.0); 200-yard games 1.0 -> 1.8 (real 2.2); 25+ carry games 12.5 -> 22.5 (real 31.8, range 24-40); 30+ 0.2 -> 2.8 (6.2).** Season: rushing leaders by season 1,509-1,954 over 16 seasons (real 1,459-2,005), 300+ carry backs 3.2 a season (real 3.0); the busiest back averages 355 carries (was 340; real 326, real max 349), and fewer committee lead backs reach 200 carries (16.9 vs real 21.4; was 17.7). Team-sport test unchanged (below-average QB 10.3 / 5.1 / 2.3 wins with stacked / average / weak casts, before 10.3 / 5.4 / 2.8; best QB on a bad roster 8.4 both). Consistency test unchanged (RB carries change 11-13% season to season vs real 14%). Season check: **25 of 282** (band 19-26).
- **Tried and not kept:** (1) the covering defender making only part of the tackles on short catches (barely moved corners, no real data to set it); (2) more weight on tackling skill (`TACKLE_SKILL_K` 0.26 with the middle linebacker 7% lower): ~7 fewer 12+ games, but 80+ tackle players 113 -> 121 and 80+ tackle DBs 57 -> 64, because it piles stops on starters (the real NFL spreads them over more players); (3) a bigger rest share for workhorse backs (`RB_SPLIT_MIN` 0.25): trims the busiest back but takes the big games away again; (4) a "hot hand" (feeding a back who's running well): real play-by-play shows none (a lead back's 2nd-half share of carries is 0.64-0.69 whether he averaged under 2.5 or over 6 yards a carry in the 1st half).
- **Checks at the end:** single games in or near real: passing 350+ 25-31 (real 30), 5-TD passing 3-4 (4.8), 150-yard receiving 26-34 (26), 3+ sacks 20-25 (16, range 10-23), 2+ INTs 19-23 (21), 4+ field goals 45-49 (44). Season check 25 of 282 (13 with known reasons); league averages unchanged (points 22.9, YPA 7.0, YPC 4.5, FG% 84.6). Nothing capped anywhere.
- **Still off / follow-ups (single-game extremes):** (1) backs score too often near the goal line (3.6% of carries vs real 3.1%; 3-rushing-TD games 9-14 a season vs real 8; season `rush_td>=8` high) - existed before this work; (2) teams try too few 50-59 yard field goals on normal 4th downs (~150 a season vs real ~205; total FG tries match); (3) end-of-half kicks with no timeout left still come at 9-24 seconds (~60-70 a season vs real ~4; real teams spike or run one more play); (4) backfield usage is a bit polarized: too few 200-250 carry lead backs, the busiest back a little high; (5) 400-yard passing games 4-7 vs real 8.8 and 12+ catch games 8-12 vs real 15.8 (the main session's earlier runs had them at 6.3 and 12.8); (6) a season benchmark by role (line + edge, off-ball linebackers) would replace the label caveats for `dl_tackles` and `edge_sacks`.

### Git and the full integration pass (Oct 23-24; Blake: "set up git with a first snapshot, then run the full integration pass (all checks plus several full seasons and offseasons in the app), and fix what breaks")
- **Git (version history):** installed with Blake's OK (`C:\Program Files\Git\cmd\git.exe`; older terminal windows don't know the `git` command until reopened). The project folder is now a git repository: every snapshot ("commit") is a saved copy of all the files that can be compared with or restored later. Not saved in it (`.gitignore`): saves, the packaged .exe build, the downloaded nflverse data, Python's cache files. Side sessions don't commit; the main session takes the snapshots. Snapshots so far: `53b0ad7` first snapshot (it caught some side-session files mid-work: draft, draft screens, sim tuning); `96a3990` the fixes below; a final one after this entry.
- **What was run:** every check (`realism_check` 16 seasons, the new `game_extremes_check`, `draft_check` 22 seasons, `free_agency_check`, `news_check`, team sport and consistency); an engine script (scratchpad `integration_seasons.py`) that plays 6 full seasons through the real game flow (startup draft, 90-man camp, preseason, final cuts, every week, every offseason phase with re-signings, offers and live draft picks for your team, save and load at every stage) and checks roster sizes, contracts, cap, depth charts and the free agent pool after each step; and the app itself (startup draft, preseason, two seasons, an offseason with the Contracts tab, scouting, a live draft pick, camp, save/load; no server errors).
- **Broke and fixed:** (1) **cap death spiral** (big): getting under the cap at week 1, a team cut a player, refilled the spot at the minimum, still counted the cut as savings, and went round after round; Nashville went down to 11 players and a game crashed with no QB. Now a cut counts only what it saves beyond a minimum replacement when the spot must be refilled, and practice squad cuts count nothing (`free_agency._comply`). (2) Two retirement headlines could be identical (two players can share a name): the headline now names the position. (3) New random-roster leagues reused the same dice ("salt") as each other. (4) The garbled "Â·" in the honors line.
- **Found and sent to the side sessions that own the files:** (1) the startup draft undervalued quarterbacks (Dallas started a QB rated 27; teams took their first QB as late as round 25): fixed by the draft screens session (entry above: every team has its QB by round 4). (2) AI teams' money: no room kept for week 1, $100M dead-money seasons, teams paying two expensive QBs: the contracts session added an affordability check to AI extensions (`free_agency._extension_fits`; its money check: dead money 4.0-4.6% of the cap, nobody over; AI extensions ~55 -> 47 a season; its own played 5-season run was still going at this entry). (3) 12+ tackle games too high: the sim session (entry above).
- **Final run on the combined code** (all three sessions' changes in): 6 seasons, **0 problems**. Team strength spread (point differential per game, 1 standard deviation; real ~6): 5.1, 5.7, 5.4, 5.1, 5.3, 6.2 (a startup-draft league starts uneven, as Blake wanted; random rosters start ~4). Starter average 64.8 -> 66.4 -> 67.3 -> 67.9 -> 67.7 -> 67.7 (settles after the startup draft's thin depth fills in). Starters with 9+ seasons 7.5 -> 9.3% (real ~9; a startup league begins young and ages into it). Each season ~50-70 s, each offseason 10-23 s. Season realism after the sim session's final tuning: 25 of 282 out of range (band 19-26); the draft and contracts changes don't touch the generated test leagues it uses.
- **Not covered (at the time of this entry):** the contracts session's played 5-season run (its report goes in its own entry); the app test save (Charlotte) was past its startup draft, so the QB fix shows only in a new game.

### Salary cap research: how the real cap grows, and cap room when free agency opens (Oct 24; Blake: "making sure that the cap in the game is realistic and scales up like in the real nfl over the years (i think it fluctuates depending on how the ratings for the league are which usually go up but research and make sure). NFL free agency is what makes the nfl so good with its cap room making teams play on a balanced playing field... i really want to make sure its perfect")
- **How the real cap is set:** players get a fixed share of league revenue, about 48% under the 2020 CBA (up to 48.8% through the "media kicker" as the new TV money arrived), minus benefits, divided by 32 teams. About 60-65% of revenue is national (mostly the TV deals, shared equally); the rest is local (tickets, sponsors). The TV deals are long (the current ones: 11 years, ~$110B, signed 2021, started 2023, league can opt out after the 2029 season; talks to redo them early started in 2026). So **TV ratings matter, but not year to year**: they set the price of the next TV deal, which then locks in steady growth for a decade. On-field quality (player ratings) plays no part. The only real drops: 2011 (new CBA, owners' share went up: -2.4%, then +0.5% and +2.0%) and 2021 (COVID, empty stadiums: -7.9%, with a negotiated floor and the loss spread over later years). The big jumps came with new TV money: 1998 +26%, 2006 +19% (new deals and a new CBA), 2022-24 +14% / +8% / +14% (new deals plus COVID money repaid).
- **Real growth 1995-2026:** 7.0% a year compounded (mean 7.4%), spread (1 standard deviation) 5.9%, from -7.9% to +26.4%; 5 of 31 years under +3%. 2015-2026: 7.0% a year, spread 5.2%. 2027: about $323-327M projected (Spotrac / OverTheCap, unofficial).
- **The game today** (`contracts.CAP_HISTORY_M`, `CAP_GROWTH`, `set_cap`): every real cap 1994-2026 is correct (2026 = $301.2M, checked against the NFL's announcement). After 2026, each year is an independent random draw: 7.0% average (right), but spread only 2.4% (real 5-6%), never below +2% or above +13%, never a drop, no TV-deal eras. 200 test leagues: the cap passes $1B around 2045 (median $1.17B in 2046; real history grew 8.7x in 32 years, so that's in line). Minimum salaries follow the 2020 CBA table to 2030, then scale with the cap (real minimums run about 0.29-0.33% of the cap, so scaling is a fair stand-in for future CBAs).
- **Cap room the day free agency opens** (scratchpad `fa_room.py`, the 5 offseasons of the integration run, AI teams, top-51 rule; real from OverTheCap via CBS Sports, March 2024 and March 2025, teams over the cap counted as ~0 since they must get under by the start of the league year): most room 32-70% of the cap (real 40%, 45%); median team **15-19% and rising each year** (real ~14% both years); lowest tenth of teams **~9%** (real ~0); teams with under 2% of the cap: **0 of 31** (real 7-8 of 32). So the game's AI teams are too even and too careful: no one arrives in "cap hell", which in the real NFL forces veterans to be cut into the market (cap casualties) and keeps contenders from buying everyone. Dead money is also on the low side (contracts session: 4.5-5.2% vs real 5-10%), the same pattern.
- **Blake's decisions (Oct 24):** (1) **"TV-deal eras"** for the cap after 2026; (2) each deal's size: **"mostly market, some buzz"** (his "ratings" idea, kept to the size it has in real life); (3) AI teams' cap room: **"Yes, match the real NFL"** (contenders go all in, some arrive at free agency with no room and must cut veterans; rebuilders sit on room).
- **Built: `engine/league_money.py`** (rules; `contracts.set_cap` asks it for each league year after 2026). A normal year grows 6.8% +/-1.3% (real between-deal years). TV deals: 7-11 seasons, signed 1-2 league years ahead (the real current deals end after 2029, so the first in-game deal starts in 2030); the new money adds extra growth of 9 +/-7 points (from -4 to +30), 70% in its first season and 30% in its second; buzz moves it by 4 points per point of buzz averaged over the seasons since the last deal (a typical average spreads about +/-0.5, so buzz is worth about +/-2 points against the market's +/-7). Labor deals every 10 years (the real one ends after 2030): owners win 35% (growth 8, 5 and 3 points lower for three years, like 2011-13), smooth 45%, players win 20% (+5 points in the first year). Shocks: 2% a year, announced the season before, the cap falls 4-9% and the lost growth comes back 50% / 30% over the next two seasons. **Next year's cap is fixed a year ahead** (`league.tv["proj"]`; the real NFL tells teams in December) and lands within about 0.6% of it; AI planning (`cap_for` of a future season) uses the projection, then the expected pace with every signed deal. Saved as `league.tv` (database MONEY_INFO "tv"). Older saves already past the 2028-29 signing window: the old deals run one more season and the new ones are signed at once.
- **Buzz** (`record_buzz`, at the end of each season): how even the league was (spread of point differential per game), one-score games, close playoff games, and big individual seasons (4,500 passing yards or 40 TD passes, 1,600 rushing, 1,500 receiving, 17 sacks, 9 interceptions), each against the game's own normal seasons (12 full seasons measured, scratchpad `buzz_sample.py`), averaged and scaled to about +/-1 a season. It only touches the TV deal's size, and it's the reason a deal story says ratings were "booming", "strong", "soft" or "slumping".
- **News** (`engine/news_money.py`, a new "League business" kind on Bitter): each league year's cap (with the change, the reason when there is one, and next year's projection), a new TV deal (years, about how much a year from `TV_VALUE_PER_CAP` 1.5x the league's total cap, how much more than the current deals pay, what ratings did), a new labor deal and who won it, a revenue shock. **Contracts tab:** under the cap line, "League money: 2030 cap projected $473M (+23.9%) · new TV deals signed for 2030-2040 · labor deal through 2030". `contracts.fmt_money` writes billions ("$1.17B"; the cap passes $1B around 2045).
- **Cap path check** (scratchpad `cap_eras.py`, 300 leagues x 40 seasons, random buzz): growth 7.3% a year on average (real 1995-2026: 7.4%), spread 4.2% (real 5.9%, most of the gap is real history's two bad events and 1998's +26% in only 31 years), drops 3.6% of years (real 6%), +12% or more 9.9% (real 13%); 4.8 TV deals in 40 years, 9 seasons long; 2046 cap median $1.2B ($970M-1.5B).
- **AI teams' approach** (`free_agency.set_approaches`, every league year): each AI team reads its window from last season's record and its best 22 players (plus its own judgment, `APPROACH_NOISE`): the top 10 go **all in** (restructure up to 12% of the cap to keep or sign starters, back-load new deals 15% a year, keep half the usual in-season room, pay 5% more for starters, extend core players 65% of the time and let next season's commitments run to 115% of next season's cap); the bottom 7 **rebuild** (pay players 29 and older 20% less, cut overpaid veterans 85% of the time instead of 60%, keep 2% extra room through the offseason). Teams that aren't rebuilding now re-sign and tag their starters keeping only what week 1 adds (they find the rookie and in-season money after the draft, as real teams do); before, every team held about 9% of the cap back for the draft, in-season moves and week 1, so no one ever arrived at free agency short. A safety floor keeps every AI signing at least week 1's extra under the cap (the first try had an all-in team $9.7M over at week 1).
- **Results, cap room when free agency opens** (`tools/free_agency_check.py`, new section; 3 generated leagues x 4 offseasons; real March 2024 in brackets): most room 40.5 -> 42.8% [39.9], 5th 25.1 -> 25.4% [27.4], median 13.8 -> 12.6% [13.2], 24th of 32 **11.0 -> 4.8%** [2.7], teams under 2% of the cap **0.1 -> 3.8 a year** [7-8 on March 2, before the teams over the cap got under it, so the true opening-day count is lower]. Also moved toward real: players moving to a new team 19.1 -> 20.2% [23.8], market deals with a new team 37.8 -> 40.6% [50-57], dead money 2.5 -> 3.5% of the cap [5-10], cap cuts 12 -> 17 a year, tags 10.7 -> 11.7 [6-14]. Cap legality 0, missing contracts 0. Tried and dropped: back-loading every AI deal 15% a year (no effect on the low end; no year-by-year real data to set it from).
- **Tool fix:** `free_agency_check` ran its final money check in the "preseason" stage, where only the top 51 count, so it always reported AI teams over the cap at week 1 (31-115 team-weeks); it now counts every contract there, as the game's final cuts do: 0.
- **Integration test** (6 seasons, startup-draft league, the real game flow, save/load at every stage, now also checking the TV and cap records survive save/load): **0 problems**. That league: 2027 +8.9%, 2028 +8.9%, 2029 +6.9% (TV deals for 2030-2040 signed, +25 points, buzz +0.58), 2030 **+22.9%**, 2031 +5.3% (owners won the new labor deal), 2032 projected flat. Room at free agency opening: median 15.4%, 25th percentile 7.4%, about 1 team a year under 2% (a startup-draft league starts with every contract fresh and even, so its spread builds over the years).
- **Watch (sent to the contracts session):** in that run the average starter rose to 69.3 by 2031 and 9+ year veterans to 10.8% (the run before: 67.7 and 9.3%). The 22-season draft check agrees: 9+ seasons 10 -> 12% (real 9%), drafted rookies among starters 9 -> 7% (real 8%), late-season starters about 1 point higher. Not from this work: with every AI-approach effect switched off the draft check gave identical numbers (it doesn't run the money side the same way). The likeliest cause is the contracts session's same-day change to retirements under contract (5.1% -> 0.82% a season, fitted to real), which keeps veterans on rosters; it's checking.
- **For later:** AI teams don't use void years (a real way to keep first-year cap hits low); the startup-draft league's free agency room starts too even (fresh contracts); in-season restructures by all-in teams to add at the trade deadline (no trades in-season yet).
- **Oct 21, made sturdier (the friend left before we learned what his PowerShell fix was):** the package is now ONE file (`--onefile`): `FrontOfficeFootball.exe` holds everything, so it also works if double-clicked inside the zip without extracting (the most likely cause of "a little loading bar and then nothing"). It starts in about 4 seconds on Blake's PC. Because a one-file .exe run from a zip is a temporary copy, the packaged game now saves to `Documents\Front Office Football\saves` (`engine/saves.py`; `python app.py` still uses the project's `saves/`). **Browser backup:** if no game screen has connected within 30 seconds (`app.on_connect`), the packaged game opens itself in the web browser (`app.py`). It watches the screen, not the window, because a test with WebView2 deliberately broken showed the window frame can appear while staying blank. **Tested:** the lone .exe with nothing beside it opens its window in 4 s and no browser tab opens; with WebView2 broken it opened in Firefox after 33 s; a new game (startup draft) saved into Documents. The friend's existing save is in the old `saves` folder next to his old .exe; to keep it with the new version he'd copy it into `Documents\Front Office Football\saves`.

### Blake's playtest notes, Oct 24: manual saving only, saves cleared, RB receiving and rushing QBs, rarer top ratings, the Draft tab
- **Saving is manual only (Blake: "remove the auto saving feature, if i close the game without saving i dont want it to still save cause then whats the point of saving").** Autosave was already off by default; what saved anyway was the recovery copy: closing the window with unsaved changes wrote `<save>.recovery.db` and the main menu offered it back. Removed: the recovery copy on closing (`app.py`, `state.write_recovery`), the main menu's "Unsaved changes found" notice and the Load list's note (`menu_page.py`), the Autosave switch (Settings now just explains saving), autosave in `state.mark_changed` and the header tooltip (`main_page.py`), the `autosave` app setting (`saves.py`). A new game is still saved once when you create it, so it shows up in Load game; saving still discards any old recovery copy. **Tested in the app** (test saves): loaded a save, opened free agency (header: "Unsaved changes: Save"), closed the game: the save file untouched (same time, still the re-sign phase), no recovery file; main menu and Settings draw, no server errors.
- **Saves cleared (Blake: "remove any game saves i have in the game start the saves i have fresh").** The six files in `saves/` (indianapolis-speed-2026 and its recovery copy, league.db with its history and recovery files, settings.json) went to the Windows Recycle Bin (recoverable, not erased). No packaged-game saves existed (`Documents\Front Office Football\saves`). Technical call: the realism tools used `saves/league.db` as their "saved" test league, so a copy is now `tools/data/realism_league.db` (`tools/simlab.SAVE_FILE`), and the reports stay comparable with earlier ones.
- **RB receiving and rushing QBs (Blake: "double check that running backs arent getting too much receiving. also are there too many rushing qbs").** 16-season realism check (generated leagues), sim vs real 2021-25: RB catches #1 82 vs 87 (74-107), 50+ catch RBs 9.5 vs 9.6, 400+ receiving-yard RBs 8.2 vs 7.6; QB rushing #1 833 vs 848 (579-1,143), 400+ yard QBs 6.2 vs 6.4, 600+ 2.6 vs 2.8: both match. Blake's own league (startup draft, 2026 through the playoffs): RB+FB share of catches 20.1% (real 2021-25: 20.2-22.5%, from nflverse player stats; Claude first said "about 17-19%", which was wrong), QBs on pace for 400+ rushing 7; but the top RBs ran high (94, 86, 86, 82, 72 catches; real #3 69, #5 60) and his own RB led his team with 86 catches for 990 yards (his best WR had 50). A 5-season startup league from the integration test: #5 RB 64-72 catches (real 60, 54-69), the rest in range. Reading: realistic overall; a bit high at the top in startup leagues, where some teams draft weak receivers and their best receiving option is the back (real: McCaffrey 2019, 116). Not changed; rerun the realism tools on startup-draft leagues (already planned) to check it there.
- **Rarer top ratings (Blake: "i want higher ratings to feel more rare and special and much less common. in fof8 it is extremely rare to have a 90+qb and i dont know if ive ever gotten a 95+ qb in my entire 14k hours"; he picked "FOF8-rare").** Claude's advice, which Blake took: change the numbers on screen, not the talent (the sim, development, the draft and the money are calibrated on the internal ratings; fewer good players would mean fewer real 5,000-yard seasons and redoing every check). `engine/scale.py`: below 70 nothing changes (median player 50, typical starter as before); above it the Oct 22 curve is stretched (`_TOP`): internal 88 shows 80 (was 88), 90 shows 84 (92), 92 shows 88 (97), 93 shows 90 (97), 95 shows 94 (98), 96 shows 96. Same for every skill, potential, scouting range and the kickers' own curve. Measured on 5 leagues (fresh and 1-5 seasons in), every rostered player: 80+ 40-82 -> 10-30 (the 42 is the inflated old test league), 90+ 7-25 -> 0-5, 95+ up to 7 -> 0; best QB 92-97 -> 84-90, QBs at 90+ 1-2 -> 0-1. Hidden judgments tuned on the old curve keep it (`scale.legacy_shown`, never shown): award voters' "film" (`awards.film`) and the news's notable/star cutoffs (`news._Ctx.caliber`), so awards and news behave exactly as tuned. Note: `tools/draft_check.py`'s "on screen 80+ / 90+" counts drop accordingly (display only).
- **The Draft tab (Blake: it "doesnt need to be up there until the offseason because you cant scout until then"; chose "hide it until offseason"; a college-season hub can come later) and the draft's look (Blake: "much better and much less like a google form", with FOF8's Amateur Draft Preview as the model):** both handed to a new side session, "Draft screens redesign, FOF8-style" (screens only).

### More pop-ups, the draft room as a window, and a less form-like look (Oct 22; side session; Blake: "can we go harder on making more things pop ups like front office 8. like the draft should be one ... and clicking players should open a window player card of them. also just more general optimizing and tuning of the ui and how it looks and feels. still feels a little clunky and too much like a google form")
- **Player names are links everywhere** (click the name: his card pops up; click elsewhere in the row: the page's own side panel, as before): Roster, Contracts (every view), Depth chart (board, spot editor, captains), Stats (leaders, player stats), the team window's roster. Already linked before: box scores, Bitter, Awards, offseason report, Home.
- **Team windows (new, FOF8's team screen):** click any team name (Standings, Schedule, Home's division box and this week/last game, Playoffs seeds, box scores, Stats leaders and team stats, the team summary's game log) for a window with its **Team summary** (record, game log, stats with league ranks, players' stats) and **Roster** (same table as the Roster page; click a player for his card). "Show on the team pages" closes it and switches the pages behind to that team.
- **The draft room is a window** (`popups.KeptWindow`, built from `main_page.py`; `draft_page.py` itself untouched, coordinated with the draft-screens session): the Draft section, "Scout the draft class" and "Go to the draft room" open it over whatever page you're on; it opens by itself when a game loads mid-draft and when the draft reaches your pick; closing it (X, Esc) and reopening keeps your place; prospect reports, trade offers and draft order stack on top. It stays built while closed, so it's always current. Exception: a new league's startup draft stays a full page (there are no other pages yet).
- **Schedule:** clicking a played game opens its summary pop-up; the empty "No game selected" panel is gone and the game list is wider.
- **The look** (`theme.DESKTOP_CSS`, one block, every line commented; Claude's calls, PROVISIONAL for Blake to react to): pages switch instantly (no sliding) and pop-ups open in under a tenth of a second; no ripple effects on buttons; button groups (Roster / Practice squad / ...) drawn as one segmented control; drop-downs square and compact; tables with a shaded header row, striped rows and a highlight under the mouse; panels with a green top edge and squarer corners, like windows; slightly darker page background (`theme.CHALK` #ECEFEA) so panels stand out; thin scrollbars. Roster page: the filter row fits on one line (practice squad switch shortened to "I run my practice squad / Staff runs it", details in its tooltip), less space above the table, and a smaller "No player selected" note that says what clicking does.
- **Code:** `screens/popups.py` (links for players, teams and games, `open_team`, `KeptWindow`, `set_view_team`, `close_all`); `roster_page.add_table_slots` (shared by the team window); small edits in `standings_page`, `home_page`, `schedule_page`, `game_page`, `playoffs_page`, `stats_page`, `contracts_page`, `depth_page`, `theme`, `main_page` (draft window, team window hook).
- **Tested in the app** (port 8099, copies of the test saves and the draft session's mid-draft save; screenshots at 1600x900 from a hidden Edge window, scratchpad `shoot.py`): team window from Home and Standings (summary, roster, a player card on top); player names on Roster, Contracts, Stats; the draft room opening by itself mid-draft, Next pick inside it (#29 -> #30), a prospect report stacked on top, Esc closing them one at a time, the Draft button and the bar button reopening it at the same pick; Sim week afterwards (bar, Schedule, game summary pop-up from the schedule); no server errors. Not tested: the startup draft page (unchanged code path), the desktop window itself (same page).
- **Decided (Blake, Oct 22):** the Contracts negotiation panel and the Roster moves panel stay side panels for now ("Keep Contracts and Roster as side panels for now"); you work through many players in a row there.

### Does talent feel like a quota? (Oct 24; Blake: "it almost kind of feels like each level of talent is filling a quota rather than a natural varied pool of talent in different positions that can vary year to year like how FOF8 was... with FOF8 sometimes the best qb potential in a startup draft would be like mid 70s")
- **How talent is made:** each draft class is the best of 2.5 generated prospects per class spot at each position (`draft.POOL`), from the same talent spread every year; a startup pool is ~140 QBs. Measured (scratchpad `class_variety.py`, 40 classes in one league, true potential on the shown scale): the best QB ceiling in a class varies a lot (51-97, spread 12.9; no 80+ QB in 19 of 40 classes), other positions much less (best T 67-82, spread 3.2; DE 4.6, CB 5.5, RB 6.4). Startup pools (8): the best QB ceiling 90-96 every time, 2+ QBs at 88+.
- **Real classes vary only as much as luck** (nflverse draft picks 2000-18, scratchpad `real_vintage.py`): year-to-year variance / mean of Pro Bowlers per class at a position QB 1.03, RB 0.84, WR 0.85, TE 0.72, OL 0.47, EDGE 0.90, DT 1.00 (1.0 = pure luck; multi-year starters the same). The game's classes (`game_vintage.py`, 60 classes, ceilings at starter and star level): 0.64-1.05. So the game already varies like the real NFL, and **class "vintages" (a hidden per-position strength per class), which Blake had picked on Claude's earlier claim that real classes are loaded or barren beyond chance, are not built**: the data says that claim was wrong (the 2004 QBs or 2017 RBs happen about as often as chance makes them). Blake declined league-wide talent eras.
- **What most likely makes FOF8 feel different:** FOF8 shows the staff's estimate, which sits nearer average when the staff is unsure (an unproven player's ceiling looks modest until it's proven), so a startup pool's best QB can read ~75 and a confirmed 80+ is special. Here every screen showed the truth. Blake chose "every rating you see is your staff's evaluation, everywhere, like FOF8" (no separate "staff view" label) and the coaching staff system; both are in the coaching staff side session, which was asked to make the number a team sees its best estimate (pulled toward average in proportion to its uncertainty), not truth plus noise (noise alone would make 90s more common), and to check that an average staff's best shown QB ceiling in a startup pool and a draft class usually lands mid-70s to mid-80s.

### Out of position, checked (Oct 24; Blake: "just making sure, you fixed players playing out of position to be realistic and accurate (obviously not for like corner to safety but like DT to qb there should be a very noticeable fall off, but it should be realistic)")
- **QB: fixed and holding** (scratchpad `oop_qb.py`, 40 games each): starter 66.5% / 6.9 YPA / 20.9 points; a DT at QB 37% / 3.3 YPA / 1.6 INT / 5 sacks / 4.5 points; a G 38% / 3.6; a WR 48% / 4.3 / 10.8 points; a RB 49% / 4.6; a K 45% / 3.9. Real benchmark: the 2020 Broncos, practice squad WR Kendall Hinton at QB, 1 of 9, 13 yards, 2 INT, lost 31-3. Gap: the emergency QB still throws ~28 times a game (the Broncos threw 9); real coaches run almost everything.
- **Everywhere else: not yet** (`oop_all.py`, 60 games, one player moved, same seeds; `oop_snaps.py` confirms he plays every snap): far moves barely hurt games (a WR at left tackle, fit 1: -1.9 points scored and 3 sacks allowed in a game; a DT at LT -0.5; a WR at CB +2.4 points allowed, +0.44 opponent YPA), because the sim's soft caps (`plays.py`: `RUSH_EDGE_CAP` on blockers, and likely the completion, target, YAC and run caps) limit how bad one player can be, which undoes the out-of-position floor. Close moves cost too much in the fit itself (CB at FS 62 -> 43, DE at off-ball OLB 68 -> 31; S at CB 78 -> 69 and G at LT 70 -> 61 look right), because every move pays the same new-position penalty (`depth.NEW_POSITION_NOW` 6) however related the positions are. Sent to the sim realism session with targets (close moves a point or two a game, far moves a lot) and the before/after checks.
- **For later (Blake): player cards with college stats and the positions a player played in college**, which affect his rating at a position he switches to (the natural hook: `p.learned`, the familiarity that already shrinks the new-position penalty). Fits with the college-season hub idea for the Draft tab.

### Coaching staff, staff-rated players (Oct 24; side session, IN PROGRESS, nothing committed; design and Blake's answers: `docs/staff-design.md`)
- **Blake's calls (Oct 24):** FOF8-sized staff of 5 coaches (HC, OC, DC, assistant HC, strength coach) plus college scouting director, pro personnel director, head trainer; owner's staff budget; development effect "in between" (~+4-5 potential over 4 years for a top-3 staff); coach ratings shown as reputation + track record (true skill hidden); AI teams judge players through their own staff's eyes; sim = coordinator style only; benching later; carousel in rounds after the season; any coach can be fired in-season (interim).
- **Real data measured (nflverse games 1999-2026):** 6.6 head coach changes a year (sd 1.8) + 1.5 in-season; tenure mean 4.1, median 3; firing odds by wins x tenure (table in the design doc). Coaching effect on development: not detectable for QBs under head coaches (upper bound ~0.025 EPA/dropback per spread); drafted players' value under the same coach: spread ~1 AV, at most ~2.
- **Built:** `engine/staff.py` (coaches, careers, budgets, firing table, the carousel, in-season firing, coaching scores, eval skill, styles), `engine/evaluation.py` (each team's view of each player: own ~1.5 / others ~3.5 internal points, best estimate pulled toward average by uncertainty, ceiling = current + shrunk growth, staged camp reveals), `scale.seen`, `screens/staff_page.py` (Staff page in the Team section, candidates and offer pop-ups); new offseason phase "coaching" (offseason.py, season.py, main_page/home labels); `league.staff` saved in league_info (older saves get one on load); development.py takes coaching (COACH_LEAN 0.95, GROWTH 0.06, FALL 0.12; PROVISIONAL); sim styles from coordinators (gameteam `_StyleDice`, same spreads); AI eyes in depth charts, cuts, in-season moves, FA bids (`free_agency._eval` vs the public view); `draft.team_skill` = college scouting director.
- **Checked:** staff harness 25 seasons x 3: HC changes 6.2-6.7/yr, tenure median 3-4, coordinators ~24%/yr (real ~29%); coaching off = identical offseason (hash); draft_check 22 seasons before/after coaching: within noise except drafting odd/even 0.67 -> 0.74 (real 0.53); potential moving 0-3 a year 88.4-88.9% (off 89.2-89.6%); best QB ceiling an average staff shows: draft classes ~70-75 (one 88), startup pools 87-93 (proven veterans; reported to the main session). In the app (port 8111, scratchpad saves): Staff page, candidates, offer, round 1 settle (hired DC, expiring director left).
- **Not done yet:** free_agency_check / draft_check (with AI eyes) / realism_check before-after were running at the end (scratchpad `checks.sh`, outputs fa_*.txt, dc_after2.txt, rc_*.txt); screens other than Staff still show true ratings (switch to `scale.seen` pending the main session's OK); startup draft views; strength coach / trainer injury effects; in-season firing hook in season.py; coach reputations run high (many "Very Good" candidates; PROVISIONAL).

### Bitter rehaul: every bit fresh and about something that happened (Oct 24, side session)
- **Blake (Oct 24):** "the tweets that are visible feel completely new and in reaction to whats happening... there should not be word for word repeats of tweets especially not in the same page/week/year". His answers: **about 80 bits a week in For you**, **snark like now**, **accounts remember their takes**.
- **Measured first** (new `tools/bitter_check.py`; 2 leagues, 3 seasons each through the real flow: startup draft, offseasons, draft): before, about 9,000 bits a season, **7,600 word-for-word repeats a season** (2,670 within a week; "53. Let's go." 84 times in one season), about 228 bits a week in For you.
- **After:** 0 word-for-word repeats in any week, season or across the save. Accounts' own bits: never the same shape (names and numbers swapped) twice in a week, at most 3 times a season. For you: about 80 bits a week, no same-shape repeats in a week. The tool fails if any of this comes back.
- **How:** reactions hand `news.py` a `news_words.Say`, and every season's bits are worded in calendar order at the end (`_Ctx.finish_posts`, `Writer.fresh`). A bit that can't be said freshly isn't posted. Wording was rewritten (`engine/bitter_words.py`) so every bit carries specifics: the star's line, the late go-ahead play and clock, comebacks, streaks, records, turnovers, sacks, rushing yards, weather, revenge or sweep of an earlier meeting, first place, who's next. Built from pieces, so ideas come out in many shapes. Award, injury, draft, move, clinch and ranking posts were converted too (`news.py`, `news_awards.py`).
- **Memory (Blake's yes):** Rex and Receipts as before; Big T comes back to lines he called out ("credit where it's due" or "same story as Week 3"); Freddie takes back a meltdown; players' guarantees get settled; fans post "book the parade" or "season's over" and later eat crow or get quoted by a rival; slump and breakout takes get follow-ups; the side that lost a first meeting posts in rematch week.
- **For you** (`bitter.FOR_YOU_PER_WEEK` 80, `FOR_YOU_MIX`; PROVISIONAL mix): per week, a share for each kind of account, then the most important; two outlet bits of the same shape (e.g. two teams' draft-class lists) don't both make it. Team and player views still show everything.
- **Stability:** bit and story ids no longer shift as weeks are played (likes stay put). Fixed older code that looked ahead (breakouts, slumps, power-ranking streak notes), and each news section keeps its own wording memory. After 3 more weeks, 0 older bits vanish and 8 of about 2,700 are reworded (left: game stories that use injury lengths known only later).
- **Speed:** the feed builds faster than before: 3.1 s vs 4.0 s and 3.3 s vs 4.7 s on the two test saves (an older career lookup in retirement stories was slow). A team or player view is about 0.03 s.
- **Tested in the app (Oct 24, resumed after the usage limit):** a save made from a test league (2029, Week 11, three seasons of history; own launch config `bitter-test`, port 8117, saves in the scratchpad). Bitter opens with the new For you (about 93-98 posts and replies a week). Simmed Weeks 11 to 14: **425 posts and replies on screen across Weeks 10-14, 0 word-for-word repeats**; the Your team view, 205 posts, 0 repeats. Memory shows up on screen (Big T: "Said it in Week 13. Saying it again: Pittsburgh can't protect." quoting his Week 13 bit). No server or browser errors. **Sim week, like for like** (same save, back to back): 4.8-5.0 s with Bitter open vs 3.1-4.7 s on Home; reopening Bitter afterwards took 0.4 s. (An earlier run showed 10-13 s a week while other sessions were running checks on this PC: noise, not the feed.) One thing seen: two quick clicks on section buttons (Home, then Bitter right away) can leave the bar showing Bitter over the Home page; a normal click fixes it. Only seen with scripted rapid clicks; not in the Bitter files.

### Out of position everywhere, not just QB (Oct 24; side session. Blake: "realistic and accurate (obviously not for like corner to safety but like DT to qb there should be a very noticeable fall off)")
- **Far moves were softened by the per-play caps.** The caps (RUSH_EDGE_CAP, TARGET_EDGE_CAP, COMP_CAP, YAC_CAP, RUN_CAP) exist to soften real players' extremes, but they also hid a receiver at left tackle. New `GameTeam.untrained`: the part of a matchup below every real player's level at that spot (only a player out of position can be there; 0 in normal play). The plays now add it outside the caps (`tuning.UNTRAINED_K` 1.0, the matchup's normal slope): pass rush and sacks (`plays._block_below`), separation for completions and target choice (`_sep_below`), receiver hands, yards after the catch, runs (`_run_below`: blockers, defenders, ball carrier, second level).
- **Untrained technique 25 -> 12** (`depth.UNTRAINED_TECHNIQUE`). Real anchor: 2020 Broncos, WR Kendall Hinton at QB went 1 for 9. At 25 a receiver or back at QB still completed 47-48% for 4.5 a throw; now 36-43% for 3.0-4.1, like a lineman there.
- **Related positions** (`depth.RELATED`, `depth.related`). Tiers follow real switch rates (nflverse rosters, switches per 1,000 player-seasons): ILB-OLB 97, C-G 80, DE-DT 78, G-T 70, DE-OLB 45, CB-S 20+, RB-FB 15; then TE-WR, FB-TE, RB-TE, S-LB, RB-WR (3-5); DL-OL, DB-WR, QB-anything under 2. Relatedness lets skills the new position partly shares start above untrained, and shrinks the too-light/too-heavy charge, since his skills already carry his size. Shown fits: CB at FS 62 -> 54 (was 43; this corner is a very light 180), S at CB 78 -> 72 (69), DE at off-ball OLB 68 -> 41 (31), TE at WR 70 -> 60 (58), G at LT 70 -> 61 (61). Far moves are lower: WR at CB 8 (16), DT at LT 11 (19).
- **Tried and dropped:** shrinking the 6-point new-position penalty for related moves. It makes CB-S nearly free, but then AI depth charts started 408-634 of 2,976 starters out of position (169 before; 202 now). Real depth charts can't settle it, since teams relabel a player's position when they move him.
- **Non-QB at quarterback: coaches run** (`GameTeam.untrained_qb_lean`, `coach.pass_probability`, `tuning.UNTRAINED_QB_RUN_K` 0.8). The real Hinton game had 11 dropbacks and 33 runs (25%); now a WR at QB has ~15 dropbacks and 45 runs, a DT or G ~12 and 44. Before, ~30 throws a game. Defenses don't yet stack the box against a team that can't pass (follow-up).
- **Game tests** (league 101, team 1, 100 games each; the moved player is taken off his own spot in both lineups, so only the target spot differs; scratchpad `oop2.py`, `oop_qb2.py`):
  - Close moves within about a point: CB at FS -1.2 allowed, S at CB -1.0, G at LT +0.6, DE at DT +0.5, TE at WR and S at MLB about 0.
  - Far moves: WR at LT, his QB sacked 2.3 -> 5.1 and hit 6.1 -> 8.8 a game, 5.2 fewer points (before: 2.1 -> 3.1 sacks); DT at LT 2.5 -> 3.6 sacks; LB at RB -1.0 YPC, -4.3 points; WR at CB +4.7 points allowed, opponents +0.9 yards a pass and +4.4% completions; RB at CB +3.9 allowed.
  - A WR at corner is bad but not a disaster, matching real cases: Troy Brown played nickel CB for the 2004 Patriots (3 INTs), and Julian Edelman covered slot receivers in 2011.
- **Talent at the spot, not his own position** (`GameTeam.talent_z(p, slot)`): rotation gaps, the running back split, the fullback/tight end leans, the kickoff run/pass lean, receiver threat and targeting, and load-the-box now judge a player away from his own position by his rating at that spot. Before, a good fullback listed behind the running back counted as an equal back and drew committee carries, and an 87-rated DT at QB counted as a great passer in the team's kickoff pass lean. Own-position players are unchanged.
- **Checks (final code):** team-sport test same character as with the changes off (below-average QB 10.6 / 5.6 / 3.2 wins with stacked / average / weak casts, off 10.6 / 5.7 / 3.2; best QB 14.7 / 11.6 / 8.3, off 13.5 / 10.7 / 8.1; great QB on a bad roster 8.3, off 7.8; differences within the test's noise of about +/-0.7 wins). The test's below-average back carries less with the changes on (223 vs 266 stacked), but that's a different player: the depth charts changed which 61-rated back it picks (endurance 49 vs 99). Consistency test matches real (tackles 14% vs 13%, WR yards 19% vs 19%, RB carries 13% vs 14%, edge sacks 32% vs 33%). AI depth charts: 202 of 2,976 starters out of position (169 before). The 16-season realism check is the main session's to run on the combined code.
- **Bug found and fixed (Oct 24, same day):** the relatedness change made the untrained-skill estimate treat a player at his OWN position as fully related (related(own, own) = 1.0). Skills his position never trains but the sim still reads, like a linebacker's ball skills when he covers a pass, came out at his overall level instead of untrained, so linebackers in coverage picked off and broke up more passes: league interceptions +8% over full seasons (0.769 vs 0.707 a game, two seed sets of 8 seasons). Found by the main session's season check, then a file-group bisect and a play-by-play trace (the same throw diverged on the covering linebacker's ball skills). Now relatedness applies only away from his own position; with the new switches off the sim reproduces the old snapshot's games exactly. Out-of-position fits and tests are unchanged.
- **Final realism (16 seasons, combined code with the fix): 24 of 282 out of range** (band 19-26; 27 with the bug). League interceptions +0.0% vs real (were +7.7%), QBs with 15+ INTs 3.9 a season (real 3.0, range 2-5; was 6.6), pass_int#1 19.6 (17.2, 15-21); points 23.2 (+2.7%), completion 65.1%, YPA 7.1, YPC 4.5. Full seasons, 8 per run, two seed sets: INT 0.733 / 0.708 a game (old snapshot 0.693 / 0.721). Still out, as before: TE receiving low, tackles>=80 and db_tackles>=80 high, rush_td>=8, plus label measures (*).
- **Resumed (Oct 24, later):** Blake's startup-pool decision "Leave it honest" recorded in `docs/staff-design.md`. Screens switched to your staff's ratings (`scale.seen` / `evaluation.user_view`): Roster (table, report, skill bars), player card (report and ratings history, moved by your staff's current read), Contracts, Depth chart and field picture, game preview, header starters average, offseason report; New game uses the league's public view (no team of yours yet). Draft page (startup room, picks lists) asked of the draft-screens session, not edited. Free agency check (101,102,103 x 4) staff off vs on was running at the end (scratchpad `fa4_off.txt` / `fa4_on.txt`); the earlier 2-league run showed one AI team over the cap at week 1 with staff on (must be 0): to chase. Still to do: draft_check with AI eyes, realism check, the main session's 6-season integration test with the "coaching" phase, carousel rounds 2-3 in the app, save/load mid-carousel.
- **Finished (Oct 24, after the reset; edits final pending the main session's realism and integration runs):**
  - **Cap slip fixed.** `free_agency_check` 101,102,103 x 4 with the staff on had 3 AI teams over the cap at week 1 (off: 0), rosters cut to 45-48: free agency compared each team's view with the league's public view, and views of different sureness are pulled toward average by different amounts, so teams overpaid their own stars. Now bids move by the staff's `evaluation.misjudgment` (its view minus what an equally unsure staff would see with no mistake; averages 0.0 for own and other players). After: cap legality 0, missing contracts 0; cap room when free agency opens most/2nd/5th/8th/median/24th 43.8/37.8/30.1/24.0/14.6/7.8% (staff off 44.7/36.2/28.3/24.3/15.2/8.8%; real 39.9/35.8/27.4/20.6/13.2/2.7%), tags 12.1 (off 12.3), extensions 45 (44).
  - **The clock** (`evaluation._clock`) ran backwards from the late free agency waves to camp; fixed (real order).
  - **Harness fix** (`tools/draft_lab.py`, one line): the cut to 53 now runs at preseason week 5 as in the game (it ran at week 1, so staffs saw only 35% of young players' camp jumps and young picks lost jobs: picks 49-64 starting in year 2 fell 43% -> 37%).
  - **draft_check, 22 seasons x 6 leagues, final (coaching + AI eyes + college director), before -> after (real):** drafting odd/even correlation 0.67 -> 0.57 (0.53); starters by pick in year 2: 1-5 79 -> 79% (80), 21-32 67 -> 62% (60), 49-64 43 -> 44% (53); R1 starter 3+ 85 -> 84% (78), R2 58 -> 57% (58), R3 36 -> 36% (38); Pro Bowlers per class 26.4 -> 25.7 (30.6); starters by position first season -> last 5 within 1-2 points as before (QB 69->71, K 75->76).
  - **Prospects' single number** (`evaluation._prospect_view`) now comes from the draft board's own reading (`scouting._reading`, the middle of the staff's range; the draft screens session's model), so the two never disagree. Average staff's best QB ceiling in draft classes: 73, 71, 77, 67, 58, 65 (truth 78, 92, 88, 71, 41, 73): a little under the main session's mid-70s-to-mid-80s target because the board's reading pulls harder; kept consistent with the board rather than tuned separately (for Blake, PROVISIONAL).
  - **Startup draft page** (`screens/draft_page.py`, OK'd by the draft screens session): board, log, selected card and roster pop-up show your staff's numbers; a startup pool is judged like other teams' players (not as prospects). Checked in the app on a copy of Blake's mid-startup-draft save (staff made on load).
  - **Carousel end to end** (engine script on the test save): round 1, save and reload mid-carousel (round 2), rounds 2-3, then "Start of the league year"; all 32 teams end with 8 coaches. Known gap: 7 AI teams end a carousel a little over their owner's budget (last-round fills and extensions); for later.
  - **Depth charts under staff eyes, measured (for the main session's passing/INT question):** realism leagues 101,102,103 + saved (128 teams): QB1 differs from the true-ratings chart on 2 teams (chosen QB 2 and 4 internal points worse); 2.8 of 25 starting spots differ per team, mostly near-ties. A/B switches sent to the sim session (staff off / AI eyes off / coach styles off).
  - **Next AI-eyes piece (not started; from the AI-views side session, `ai-views-of-pros.md` in the shared notes folder, `tools/ai_views_check.py`, `docs/research/real_own_vs_other.py`):** still on true ratings: free agency's roles and upgrade size (`depth_lists`, `willingness`), option / re-sign / extension role checks, `set_approaches`, `draft.team_needs` / `qb_situation`, game-day actives and the emergency QB, `draft.sign_undrafted`. Real data (2,409 veteran deals 2014-21): new-team signings bust more than re-signs mainly for players with little tape. Recommended: other teams' mistake scaled by last two seasons' snap share (under 30% about 6, 30-60% about 4, 60%+ about 2.5), own 1.5, shared 0.4; fix the roles-on-truth gap first, then retune on 8 leagues. Also for the free agency owner: signed veterans play 59% of snaps vs real ~45%, even with true ratings.
- **Realism A/B for the main session's regression (Oct 24; 16-season `realism_check`, frozen copy of the code from just after its 35-out run, staff features switched in-process by scratchpad `run_frozen.py`):** out of range (band 19-26), replicate 1 (saved,101,102,103) / replicate 2 (104-107): staff on 31 / 29, staff fully off 25 / 34, coach styles off 39 / 32, AI eyes off 29 / 31 (totals over both: on 60, off 59). The listed regressions are there with the staff fully off: QBs with 15+ INTs on 6.4 / 7.2 vs off 6.8 / 7.2 (real 2-5), league INTs +6-9% in every mode, 80+ tackles 117 / 113 vs 116 / 115, 12+ sacks 12.3 / 10.4 vs 12.0 / 12.9; player-games lost to injury 115-136 per team in every mode, the same as before the latest changes (the main session's pre-change report: 114-133). So they come from elsewhere, not the staff. The one staff-linked hint: the top tight end's catches run lower whenever staffs exist (on 98 / 95, off 103 / 110; real 107-126; a known gap before the staff, ~100), not fixed: inside noise per replicate, worth watching.

### Cap room at free agency's opening, measured fairly (Oct 22; Blake: "shouldnt it be getting more tuned to be more accurate, 24th of 32 and teams with almost no room are not very close")
- **The comparison was uneven.** The game measured room before any free agent signed; the real numbers were OverTheCap on March 2, 2024, eleven days before teams had to be under the cap, with teams still over counted as 0. Claude found OverTheCap's own page for the day after each league year opened (Internet Archive: March 14, 2024 and March 13, 2025), which includes the first day's signings. `tools/free_agency_check.py` now also measures room right after the first wave and compares it with those two days (`REAL_FA_DAY1`).
- **Result, current settings (3 leagues x 4 offseasons) [real 2024 | 2025]:** most room 37.7% [28.2 | 36.1], 2nd 32.9 [26.6 | 29.7], 5th 22.4 [17.9 | 21.8], 8th 18.1 [13.4 | 17.3], median 10.4 [6.4 | 11.6], 24th 5.6 [1.4 | 5.9], teams under 2% of the cap 3.3 a year [10 | 2]. Between the two real years at almost every rank (close to 2025; the top slightly roomy). Cap legality 0. The two real years differ a lot themselves (24th 1.4% vs 5.9%), so this is where the game should sit.
- **Tried, not kept** (scratchpad `fa_variant.py`, knobs changed in-process): 14 all-in teams; next-season limits 1.05 / 1.25 (identical to now: the limits never bind); no rebuild savings; bigger all-in restructures 0.16. None moved the low end more than noise (24th 5.6-5.9% after the first wave).
- **Also today:** the open-roster-spots term added to the re-sign and tag floors (a fix for a team that ended over the cap at week 1) pushed the low end away from real (0.8 teams under 2% a year), so it was taken back out; the AI-eyes session's fix (short teams fill only with players they can afford, else street free agents) handles that case: cap legality 0.
- **Tuned toward five real years (Blake: "if its realistic to tune it that way then yes").** More real data first: OverTheCap just after the league year opened in 2021 (March 19), 2022 (March 17) and 2023 (March 17), added to `REAL_FA_DAY1` with 2024-25; the target is the 2022-25 average (2021 left out: the COVID year, when the cap fell). Real 2021 / 2022 / 2023 / 2024 / 2025 [4-year target]: most room 23.9 / 21.3 / 20.0 / 28.2 / 36.1% [26.4], 5th 15.9 / 15.4 / 10.1 / 17.9 / 21.8 [16.3], median 4.8 / 6.6 / 7.3 / 6.4 / 11.6 [8.0], 24th 0.7 / 3.2 / 4.1 / 1.4 / 5.9 [3.7], teams under 2% 12 / 8 / 3 / 10 / 2 [5.8]. So 2025 (after two big cap jumps) was the roomiest of the five, and the game matched it: AI teams were roomier than a typical year at every rank, not just the low end.
- **Kept** (engine/free_agency.py, marked "Oct 22"): first-wave offers per team 3 -> 5 and second 3 -> 4, `UPGRADE_K` 0.06 -> 0.10, `IN_SEASON_BUFFER` 0.035 -> 0.025, `REBUILD_KEEP` 0.02 -> 0, `REBUILD_OLD_PAY` 0.8 -> 0.9. Room after the first wave, two league sets (101-103 and 104-106): most 37.7-38.3 -> 29.4-30.6%, 5th 22.4-23.4 -> 19.2-19.3, median 10.4-11.4 -> 9.5-10.0, 24th 5.6-6.5 -> 5.9-6.2, under 2% 2.2-3.3 -> 2.0-2.5 a year; cap legality 0 and missing contracts 0 in both; dead money 6.0%, tags 11.2-11.3, cap cuts 35-38.
- **Tried, not kept:** more extensions (`EXTEND_CHANCE` 0.65, all-in 0.85): an AI team stuck over the cap at every checkpoint; all-in teams back-loading harder (`ALLIN_RISE` 0.30, `ALLIN_RESTRUCTURE` 0.18, 12 all-in teams): 5 over-cap checkpoints on one league set and only +0.5-1 near-broke team a year.
- **Known gap:** the low end (24th ~6% vs 3.7, near-broke teams ~2-2.5 vs 5.8 a year). It needs teams that truly spend themselves into a corner, and the game has a weak spot that blocks it: a team whose money is mostly restructured bonus can reach a state where no cut saves anything, so it can't get compliant (real teams always escape: one more restructure, void years, trades). Fix that first (let `_comply` always find a way out, e.g. void-year restructures), then let contenders commit harder.
- **Head coach changes: the real spread, not just the average (Blake, Oct 24: "there can be high and low versions of that that average out... it should feel like a real fully intelligent nfl simulation that is reactive not predetermined"; he chose to tighten the yearly swing to the real one without changing the average).** Real 1999-2025: 6.6 changes a year, spread 1.8, never under 3 or over 10, steadier than 32 independent coin flips (about 2.3). Before: the game's yearly count swung 0-12 (spread 2.2-2.4; about 10% of seasons outside 3-10). Now a hiring-market brake (`staff.MARKET_K` 0.8, `MARKET_LIFT` 1.18): AI teams decide in random order, each one's odds x e^(-0.8 x (jobs opened so far - expected so far)), with this year's expected retirements (by the head coaches' ages) counted against the actual ones, so a cluster of retirements calms the firings. A busy year gets quieter as it goes and a quiet one livelier, as when owners weigh the candidate pool. Every team still rolls its own odds from its record and its coach's tenure, so surprises stay (some 4-13 coaches survive, some 9-8 coaches go). Staff harness, 6 leagues x 40 seasons: 6.78 a year (real 6.6), spread 1.73-1.84 (1.8), 0.8% of seasons outside 3-10 (real 0 in 27); coordinators now change 30-31% a year (real ~29%); head coach tenure mean 3.7, median 3 (real 4.1 / 3). PROVISIONAL mechanism (the real reason for the steadier count isn't measured, only the count).

### Faster sims, same results (Oct 22; Blake: "is there any optimization you can do so that the game sims faster/is overall faster? without degrading the game obviously")
- **Rule kept:** only changes that give exactly the same results. Every change was checked by running the same save with the same dice on the old code and the new code and comparing everything: every score and box score, every roster move, every contract, where every player is, every player's ratings. All identical. (To compare the offseason, the test script also feeds a fixed seed to the few places that roll truly random dice when no seed is given, the new season's schedule and some staff code; the game itself still rolls them randomly.)
- **Where the time went (a 2029 league, about 5,300 players counting retired ones):** half of each regular-season week was AI teams' roster moves, not the games. Biggest pieces: looking up a team's roster by walking the whole list of players (retired players included, so it slows as a league ages); working out a team's cap room again for every free agent it might sign; and a depth chart's "how good is he at this spot" working out his ceiling and notes too, then throwing them away. In the offseason, AI teams judging free agents through their staff's eyes redid the same bookkeeping (who is on the staff, who is in the draft class) hundreds of thousands of times.
- **Changes:** `depth.rating_at` works out only "now" (same arithmetic as `spot_fit`), and the sim's out-of-position penalty uses it; `League.in_order` finds a team's 53, practice squad and reserve lists from the roster lists instead of scanning every player (same order); `transactions.free_agent_pool` works out the team's cap once per call (`free_agency.AffordCheck`, same answers as `affordable`); `transactions._upgrade` builds its free agent and practice squad lists once instead of once per position; `evaluation`: the draft class set is remembered, the staff lookup takes only the jobs it needs, `misjudgment` reuses `seen_skills`' lookup, and the league-wide part of a staff's mistake (the same dice for every team) is rolled once per player instead of once per team.
- **Results (same machine, same saves, other sessions running at the same time, so expect some noise):** 4 regular-season weeks 16.5 s -> 8.9 s (about 1.85x faster); the offseason from re-signing through week 1 59 s -> 41 s, free agency waves about 4.5 s -> 2 s each. In the app a week sims in about 3.2 s including the screen refresh; the Bitter page opens in 0.5 s.
- **Looked at, left alone:** next season's schedule takes about 4-5 s (a search for a fair schedule; a faster version of its scoring gave no real gain, so it was undone). The game sim itself is now about half of a week; it's spread thin over many small pieces, with no single hot spot.
- **News was the next slow spot:** news and Bitter were rewritten from every season's records after each week (about 2.3 s after a week in 2029, more each season), and an old story's wording could shift as players' ratings changed. Fixed the same day, below.

### AI teams decide through their own staff's eyes, everywhere; the size of their mistakes from real data (Oct 22; AI-eyes side session, started by the coaching staff session. Blake, Oct 24: AI teams judge players through their own staff's eyes; Oct 22: "match real data" for how far off they are, "free agency first")
- **Switched from true ratings to each team's staff view:** free agency's roles and upgrade size, i.e. "is he better than our starter, and by how much" (`free_agency.team_score`: the league's market score moved by the team's misjudgment of him, now and ceiling, used for the free agent and its own players alike; `depth_lists`, `willingness`, the market's upgrade order); fifth-year option, extension and re-sign role checks; `set_approaches` (each AI team reads its window from its own view of its roster); `draft.team_needs`, `qb_situation` (the "QB over a good young starter" rule) and `sign_undrafted`'s weakest player; game-day actives' tiebreak and the emergency QB (`roster_rules`, rating passed from `sim/gameteam.py`). The prices stay the league's consensus (`cap_market`), as before. A regular-season week takes the same time (3.85-3.94 s vs 3.72-3.92 s).
- **Technical call (PROVISIONAL):** role decisions use the misjudgment (the staff's luck), not its view as such. Its own players, seen surely, are barely pulled toward average, other teams' players more, and a role decision compares the two. That's the same reason bids use it (the staff session's cap slip, Oct 24).
- **Mistakes on other teams' players now depend on tape** (the AI-views session's real data: 2,409 veteran deals 2014-21; teams signing another team's part-timer misjudge him far more than its full-time starter). `evaluation.OTHER_SD_BY_TAPE`: by his share of his team's snaps over his last two seasons, about **8** points for a player who barely played (15%), **5** for a part-timer (45%), **3** for a full-time starter (80%+); other teams' rookies **4.9** (unchanged; the real deals had no rookies). Before: a flat 3.5. Each season's snap shares are kept at the end of the season (`evaluation.record_tape`, saved in `league.staff["tape"]`); a league with no season on record (a new league, an older save) reads them from the depth charts (starters 85%, first backups 30%, others 5%).
- **Sizing** (`tools/ai_views_check.py`, the game measured the way the real data was; 16 leagues x 3 offseasons pooled per setting, about 11,000 market deals, on the final code; one 8-league run moved by several points, so runs are now pooled with `--save` / `--load`). Busts are players paid like a starter (top 40% of price) but under 25% of snaps the next season. Other-team signings minus re-signings [real]:

  | setting | busts, little tape | part-time | full-time | all | price-snaps correlation, own minus other |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | 6 / 4 / 2.5 | +1 | -3 | 0 | -1 | 0.07 |
  | **8 / 5 / 3 (chosen)** | **+10** | **+3** | **-1** | **+1** | **0.08** |
  | 10 / 6 / 3.5 | +9 | +1 | 0 | +1 | 0.04 |
  | real | +8 | +6 | +2 | +5 | 0.13 |

  With 8/5/3, little-tape busts are 17% own / 27% other (real 24/32). Before any of this (old code, 4 leagues) the gap at little tape was +7 but both levels far too low (5% / 12%).
- **What no size of mistake fixes:** busts run low for re-signings and new-team signings alike (all: own 9%, other 10%; real 12 / 17), because signed veterans play more than real ones (59% of the signing team's snaps vs real 43-47%), even with true ratings. That's how roles and playing time work, a follow-up for whoever owns free agency and depth (first noted by the AI-views session).
- **Cap legality fixes found on the way** (each agreed with the main session): (1) `offseason.fill_short_positions` signed the best free agent at a short spot without checking money, and `free_agency.sync` then paid him in-season terms. In `free_agency_check` one capped-out team (league 103, 2029) went from +5.4% to -18.6% of the cap, cut to 44 players and was still 0.7% over at week 1. Now it takes only free agents it can afford, counting the fills it has already made (`free_agency.asking_hit`; `affordable(..., also=)`), else a street free agent. (2) The main session's open-roster-spots room was pulled into a helper (`free_agency._open_spots_share`); it tried that room in the re-sign and tag floors too, which left about 2.5 fewer capped-out teams a year (away from real), so those two floors were put back.
- **Checks on the final code** (before the main session's later free agency knob change, Oct 22 evening): `free_agency_check` 101,102,103 x 4: cap legality 0, missing contracts 0. Cap room when free agency opens: most / 2nd / 5th / 8th / median / 24th = 42.9 / 38.2 / 29.5 / 23.9 / 14.5 / 7.3% [real 39.9 / 35.8 / 27.4 / 20.6 / 13.2 / 2.7], under 2% of the cap 4.0 a year [7-8]. After the first wave: median 10.4% [6.4 | 11.6], 24th 5.6% [1.4 | 5.9], under 2% 3.3 [10 | 2]. Tags 10.3, cap cuts 35, dead money 5.8%. Before this work (same check): 43.8 / 37.8 / 30.1 / 24.0 / 14.6 / 7.8%, 2.7 a year.
- **`draft_check`** (22 seasons x 6 leagues), before -> after [real]: round 1 starter 3+ seasons 84 -> 83% [78], R2 57 -> 56% [58], R3 36 -> 35% [38]; Pro Bowlers per class 25.7 -> 25.7 [30.6]; the QB-over-a-young-starter rule unchanged (young_good 0% QBs in round 1, other 16%); best TE after round 3 in 12 -> 8% of drafts; drafting odd/even correlation 0.57 -> 0.61 [0.53]. **Watch:** picks 49-64 starting in year 2 fell 44 -> 39% [53] (year 1 35 -> 32% [38]). It was already short; it could be noise, or veterans signed on overrated views now holding their jobs.
- **Not changed, left for later:** cap cuts' "worth" is still the consensus price (no misjudgment); the startup room's AI boards keep their own scouting model.
- **Main session to run:** the 16-season realism check and the 6-season integration test on the combined code (asked Oct 22).

### Smarter AI front offices in the draft (Oct 22; draft session. Blake: "is there more tuning and perfecting we can do for the draft and maybe specifically the ai drafting and making them very smart and real feeling (at least the really good coaches/staffs (not always though there can be unexpected misses))", then "Build all 7")
- **New real data first** (`docs/research/draft_habits.py` -> `real_draft_habits.json`, nflverse trades and picks): draft-time trades 2010-26 ~31 a draft counting deals with future picks; a team's habit lasts (net trade-downs per draft, odd vs even years, correlation 0.60; Minnesota +1.4 a draft, New Orleans -1.3; sd 0.61 across teams) but has nothing to do with drafting well (correlation with value vs slot 0.01); QBs are 33% of trade-ups into the top 10 (21% of those picks) and 13% into picks 11-32 (4.5%). Checked and ruled out (scratchpad `real_prefs_runs.py`): position runs (same position back to back in rounds 1-2: 13.0% real, 13.0% with the picks shuffled) and lasting position preferences (rounds 1-3, odd vs even years: negative at every position; need flips them year to year).
- **Built (`engine/draft.py`, `engine/scouting.py` for your staff's view):** (1) a team's need check ("would he beat my weakest starter?") uses its own view of the prospect, not his true ratings; (2) speed read like real teams (`SPEED_PRICE`: priced in at WR/DB/edge/LB/QB; at RB/TE only sharp staffs see a little, `SPEED_SHARP`); (3) a stronger age discount (`AGE_VALUE` -0.28 -> -0.60); (4) weak scouting staffs add to the league's shared mistake more gently than good ones see through it (`TEAM_INSIGHT_BAD` 0.15; the startup draft keeps the old slope, so its strong/weak team spread is unchanged); (5) big misreads (`CRUSH_*`: ~1 in 125 prospects per board, 65% a crush, 35% a blind spot, slightly rarer for good staffs), with the everyday noise lower in the class trimmed (`TEAM_MISS_DEPTH` 0.50 -> 0.25) since big reaches already ran a bit high; (6) trade habits (`trade_style`, seeded by the head coach, N(0,1), `TRADE_STYLE_K` 0.5; base `TRADE_CHANCE` lowered to keep the volume); (7) QB trade-ups (`QB_TRADE_UP` 3.0 urge and `QB_TRADE_CHANCE` 2.5 when the target is a QB the team needs). `tools/draft_check.py` has a new section 13 (front offices) and shows age relative to the average (the real ratios average ~1.11).
- **Results (`tools/draft_report.txt`, 6 leagues x 22 seasons; before -> after | real):** drafting skill lasts 0.61 -> 0.56 | 0.53; worst team 0.51 -> 0.63 | 0.74, best 1.58 -> 1.54 | 1.58; trade habits last 0.16 -> 0.44 | 0.60 (spread 0.35 -> 0.59 | 0.61); habit vs drafting value -0.01 -> -0.05 | +0.01; QBs among top-10 trade-up targets 13% -> 29% | 33% (picks 11-32 5% -> 15% | 13%); 40 time vs beating the slot WR +0.20 -> -0.02 | +0.01, DE +0.12 -> +0.05 | 0, DB +0.07 -> -0.02 | 0, LB +0.27 -> +0.17 | +0.15, QB +0.21 -> +0.11 | +0.10, RB +0.11 -> +0.12 | +0.25, TE +0.20 -> +0.17 | +0.23; age 24+ (relative to the average) 0.67 -> 0.81 | 0.79; big reaches 10.0 -> 9.0 a draft | ~8 (2023: 7 of 89 matched; biggest 49 vs 33 in that one draft); trades 19.3 -> 23.8 a draft | 21.1 (pick-for-pick). Unchanged: pick-band outcomes (picks 1-5 start 3+ seasons 95% | 93%, R1 83% | 78%, R3 36% | 38%), QB rules (0% with a young good starter), need effects, rookies 7% of starters (8%), every position's level within ~2 points over 22 seasons (TE +4).
- **Tested in the app's engine** (scratchpad `room_test2.py`, no screens): a new class (speed notes on 362 of 440 prospects), your pick at 24 with three trade-up offers, save and reload mid-draft identical, accepting an offer, sim to your next pick, staff pick, the rest of the draft.
- **PROVISIONAL for Blake:** trade habits come with the head coach because the game has no GM yet (D14). D15 decided (Blake, Oct 22: "whatever is realistic"): kept at ~1 in 125, it matches real reaches. **Gaps:** trade habits last 0.44 vs real 0.60 (real habits likely follow GMs, who stay longer than head coaches); the worst drafting team is still worse than any real one (0.63 vs 0.74); RB speed is less of an edge than in the real NFL (+0.12 vs +0.25); trades in picks 65-128 a bit frequent (8.5 vs 6.2 a draft); deals with future picks are still not in the game (D3).

### Finished seasons' news is kept as it was (Oct 22; Blake: "Yes, remember finished seasons' news so it stays fast. Old stories should stop changing once a season is over.")
- **How:** when a season ends (`season.begin_offseason`, after the last moves and before the new league year, while its games are still loaded) `news.freeze_season` writes the season's stories and Bitter bits one last time and keeps them in the save (`league.news_archive`, compressed text in `league_info`; plain JSON, no pickle, so a save file can never run code). It also keeps what the writing carries into later seasons: wording already used (no word-for-word repeats across seasons), accounts' takes to come back to, guarantees, which headlines and ids are taken. `news.build` starts from there and writes only the seasons after it.
- **Better than before:** a finished season keeps its game stories and the bits about its games. Before, they only showed for a past season when you picked it on the Bitter page (the games were read back from the history file), and its other news was rewritten every week with today's ratings.
- **Checked** (scratchpad `news_freeze_test.py`, a 2031 league played to the end of the season): building right after the freeze gives exactly what was shown before it; the kept state survives packing exactly; save/load keeps the archive; 2031's 4,005 stories (348 game stories) and 2,709 bits are the ones written at its end; no bit repeats word for word across 2031-2032; no wording errors. In the app: the Bitter page for 2031 shows the season review, the Glory Bowl story and its fans' bits; no server errors.
- **Speed:** mid-season news build 0.8 s (was about 2.5 s with 6 seasons, and growing every season); at the new league year 0.2 s. Freezing takes about 0.5-1 s once a season (3.6 s the first time in a save from before this, which freezes all its seasons at once).
- **Size:** about 2 MB for 6 seasons compressed (a 20 MB save). The 5 seasons before 2031 in that test save were kept without game stories, as they showed by default before.
- **Saves from before this:** at their next season end, every earlier season is kept as it reads then (without game stories for seasons whose games are only in the history file). Blake's saves were cleared on Oct 22, so this only affects test saves.
- **Integration test (6 seasons, code just before this change):** 0 problems; point-diff spread 6.15 / 5.74 / 4.91 / 5.13 / 4.60 / 5.66 (real about 6); 9+ year veterans 9.0 -> 11.2% (known gap, real ~9).
- **Follow-up: the gaps closed (Blake: "then lets get that stuff still short up to speed and tuned").** (1) The best/worst drafting team was compared unfairly: the sim's came from 192 teams pooled from 6 leagues, the real from one 32-team league; now each league's best and worst, averaged (worst 0.63 pooled = 0.69 per league; real 0.74). `TEAM_INSIGHT_BAD` 0.15 -> 0.10. (2) Trade habits: half the organization's, half the head coach's (`STYLE_ORG_SHARE` 0.5, `TRADE_STYLE_K` 0.4), since real habits outlast coaches. (3) RB/TE speed: average staffs now under-rate fast backs and tight ends (`SPEED_PRICE` RB -0.35, TE -0.10; real teams over-rate big, slower backs), sharp staffs correct some of it. (4) Trade odds by pick range re-set (`TRADE_CHANCE`), and QB trade-ups made sturdier (`QB_TRADE_UP` 4.0, `QB_TRADE_CHANCE` 4.0: between two otherwise identical runs QB trade-ups into the top 10 had swung 26 -> 10 of ~90). **Final (6 x 22, sim | real):** drafting skill lasts 0.52 | 0.53; each league's best/worst team 1.48 / 0.68 | 1.58 / 0.74; trade habits last 0.65 | 0.60 (spread 0.72 | 0.61), habit vs value +0.07 | +0.01; QBs among top-10 trade-up targets 28% | 33% (11-32: 14% | 13%); 40 time vs beating the slot RB +0.24 | +0.25, TE +0.23 | +0.23, LB +0.18 | +0.15, WR/DB/DE about 0 | 0; age 24+ 0.76 | 0.79; big reaches 8.4 | ~8 a draft; trades 21.6 | 21.1 a draft, by pick range 0.7 / 2.8 / 5.5 / 6.8 / 5.8 | 0.8 / 2.7 / 5.6 / 6.2 / 5.8. Pick-band outcomes, QB rules, needs and every position's level unchanged. Left: the best teams draft a little less well than real's best (1.48 vs 1.58) and the middle half sits a bit low (0.87-1.13 vs 0.96-1.16; real slot ratios average above 1, partly a measuring difference); the biggest single reach per draft (47) is above the one real draft on file (33); future-pick deals still not in the game (D3).
### A much bigger name pool, from real NFL rosters (Oct 22; Blake: "i want to massively expand the name pool to be very large and varied")
- **Source:** every player on a real NFL roster 1999-2025 (`tools/data/roster_*.csv.gz`, nflverse), once each: 15,949 players, the first name he goes by ("Chris", not "Christopher") and his last name. `tools/make_names.py` counts them into `engine/name_data.py` (4,029 first names, 6,844 last names; players who entered from 2012 on count double so the pool leans toward today's names). Before: 163 first and 175 last names.
- **How it's drawn:** `names.FIRST_NAMES` / `LAST_NAMES` list each name as many times as real players had it, so every `rng.choice` in the game (players, coaches, GMs, Bitter accounts) draws names as often as a real locker room has them: Chris, Michael and Josh common; Ja'Marr, Rejzohn or Storm rare. New players use `names.person(rng)`; first and last are drawn separately, so now and then a made-up player shares a real player's full name (Blake: fine by chance, as with real people). `names.elder(rng)` gives coaches and GMs first names from players who entered before 2008 (a 60-year-old head coach isn't a Mekhi); the AI GM session was asked to switch `staff.py` and `gm.py` to it.
- **Check (2,600 generated players):** 1,117 different first names and 1,618 last names; 14 full names repeated (the real NFL has the same: two Josh Allens, several Mike Williamses). Existing saves keep their players' names; new leagues, draft classes and coaches use the new pool.
### Startup draft position values fitted to real contract rankings (Oct 22; Blake: "are wrs overvalued in the draft? after qbs go theres a surprising amount of wrs in the startup draft", then "re-tune the startup draft's position values to the real contract rankings")
- **What a startup draft should look like:** 32 teams picking from every established player at once has no exact real version; the closest is how real teams rank established players by what they pay them. `docs/research/startup_value.py` (OverTheCap via nflverse): every veteran contract in force each season 2015-2022 (not rookie deals, which are set by draft slot), ranked by cap share; for each position the rank of its 1st, 3rd, 5th, 10th, 20th and 32nd player (median of the 8 seasons) -> `docs/research/real_startup_value.json`. Groups the game's way: edge = DE, interior = DT, off-ball linebackers = OLB + ILB.
- **Answer to Blake's question:** receivers weren't overvalued. The top WRs went a bit late (5th WR at pick 52, real 38) and depth receivers came in bunches in rounds 3-5 (picks 97-160: 15-19% WRs, real 8%), because a team's third receiver counted as a full starter. The bigger misses were elsewhere: DTs (5th at 105, real 41), RBs (5th at 358, real 156) and edge rushers (57, real 29) too late; corners (5th at 40, real 66) and safeties too early. QBs in round 1 were about right (60-64% vs real 60%; I first said "too many" from a single 2022 snapshot, which was wrong).
- **Changed (`engine/draft.py`):** `STARTUP_POS_WEIGHT` re-fitted (QB 3.0, RB 1.0 -> 1.46, WR 1.1 -> 1.32, TE 1.0 -> 1.12, T 1.2 -> 1.18, G 1.0 -> 1.11, C 1.1 -> 1.07, DE 1.2 -> 1.46, DT 1.0 -> 1.26, OLB 0.95 -> 1.09, ILB 1.1 -> 1.26, CB 1.03 -> 1.09, S 1.03 -> 1.10), and a new `STARTUP_STARTER_SHARE`: a team's third starting receiver is worth 0.65 of a full starter and its third corner 0.9 (real teams pay slot receivers and nickel corners far less than outside starters). Fitted by damped rounds on 10 test leagues (scratchpad `tune_startup.py`), checked on 10 different ones (`startup_vs_real.py`): error (rms log ratio of game pick to real rank) 0.47 -> 0.23.
- **After, game / real (pick of the k-th player at the position, 10 leagues):** QB 20th 34.5 / 34; DE 1st 18 / 18, 5th 32 / 29, 20th 133 / 146; WR 1st 18 / 16, 10th 56 / 55, 20th 103 / 122; DT 1st 26 / 19.5, 5th 63 / 41; CB 5th 55 / 66, 20th 177 / 182; S 5th 117 / 110; LB 5th 110 / 113; G 5th 125 / 118; T 5th 63 / 60. Round 1: QB 60% (real 60), edge 14% (16), WR 14% (6), DT 3% (12).
- **Still off (known gaps):** the top running backs still go late (3rd RB at pick 195, real 105) while RB depth is about right: the game's best backs don't stand out from the rest by rating as much as real top backs do in pay; tight end and center depth goes late (20th TE at 563, real 319: one starter each, and backups are worth a quarter of a starter); the 32nd WR goes at 175 (real 242); top DTs still a bit late. Pay is a proxy for value (market quirks like the 2022 receiver boom are in it), so the fit stops at "close", not exact.

### The draft plays out live, pick by pick from #1 (Oct 22; side session. Blake, playtest: "for the draft, it shouldnt just be skip pick/teleports to your pick. It should start at 1st overall and then you should be able to select a draft speed. This is how you keep up with the draft and how its going and how your board is stacking up, you watch pick by pick until its your turn.")
- **What changed for Blake:** Next: start the draft now opens the draft room at pick 1 with nothing picked (before, the AI had already picked up to your pick).
